Lawsuit Against New York AG Letitia James over Letter Alleging Group's Event Was Connected to "White Nationalist Ideals" …

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

can go forward on First Amendment coercion, First Amendment retaliation, and defamation grounds, a federal district court concludes.

From Magistrate Judge Kenneth Schroeder's Report and Recommendation Sept. 22 in Doyle v. James, affirmed Wednesday by Judge John Sinatra (W.D.N.Y.), plaintiff's allegations:

Plaintiff Paul Doyle ("Doyle") is the Senior Pastor at Cornerstone Church in Batavia, New York. Cornerstone is a small, rural church with a history of service and community outreach. Cornerstone has worked to build relationships with communities of color and was aggressive in their assistance to victims of the mass shooting in Buffalo.

Plaintiff Clay Clark ("Clark") is the owner and operator of The Thrive Time Show, a highly successful business podcast. Clark has twice been named the US Small Business Association Entrepreneur of the Year and is the recipient of other business awards.

"ReAwaken America" is a conservative political movement that hosts events throughout the country. The ReAwaken America Tour ("the Tour") was scheduled to host an event in Rochester, New York on August 12, 2022 at the Main Street Armory. However, after community members and local politicians spoke out against the event, the Armory cancelled it.

The Tour then reached out to Doyle to see if Cornerstone would be willing to host the event. Doyle met with groups from Rochester to try to understand their concerns and concluded that "the Tour was not what was being portrayed in the media and by government officials." Doyle thus agreed to host the event at Cornerstone "due to his strong belief that free speech should not be restricted and those who attempt to exercise it should not be intimidated, particularly by government officials." The event was scheduled for August 11-13, 2022.

On August 3, 2022, New York Attorney General Letitia James sent a letter addressed to Clark and General Michael Flynn—a co-organizer of the Tour—to Doyle's home address "c/o Cornerstone Church." Plaintiffs allege that the letter was "nothing less than an attempt to covertly intimidate and threaten the Plaintiffs into shutting down the event."

As New York's top law enforcement officer, I have significant concerns that the ReAwaken America Tour's upcoming event at the Cornerstone Church in Batavia, New York on August 12 and 13 could spur extremist or racially motivated violence. These concerns center around the event's proposed dates, which coincide with the five-year anniversary of the deadly Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville, Virginia, and past extremist statements made by yourselves and the other featured speakers on the tour.

I am especially concerned about featured speakers' regular allusions to white nationalist ideals connected to the "Great Replacement Theory," a conspiracy theory that warns of white genocide and efforts to replace native born Americans with immigrants. The theory is frequently linked to violent actions, including the racially motivated mass shooting that killed 10 people at a Tops Friendly Markets store in Buffalo. Especially in light of this racist mass shooting, and other recent episodes of racially motivated violence in New York and throughout the country, the Office of the Attorney General is concerned that such rhetoric could contribute to violent or unlawful conduct at the ReAwaken America Tour's upcoming event….

The Office of the Attorney General writes to remind you that New York law prohibits racially motivated violence, harassment, or interference with another person in the exercise of their civil rights. New York Civil Rights Law § 79-n empowers the Office of the Attorney General to investigate acts of violence, intimidation, threats, or harassment directed at people based on a belief or perception regarding an individual's race, color, national origin, ancestry, gender, religion, religious practice, age, disability, or sexual orientation. In addition to actual damages, any person who violates this statute can be held liable for $5,000 in penalties for each violation.

Additionally, New York Civil Rights Law § 40-c prohibits discriminating against another person in the exercise of their civil rights—including their right to peacefully protest—based on similar protected characteristics. Finally, New York Executive Law § 63(12) empowers the Office of the Attorney General to take action against any business engaged in significant fraud or illegality—including the violation of New York's civil rights laws.

The Office of the Attorney General has a duty to protect New Yorkers from extremist and racially motivated violence. We stand ready to investigate any violation of the laws above and, if necessary, to enforce them to the fullest extent available. You are therefore instructed to take all necessary steps to ensure that the event complies fully with the requirements of New York's civil rights laws and all other applicable state and federal statutes.

Your cooperation in ensuring a peaceful and law-abiding event will be greatly appreciated.

Plaintiffs allege that James's "inferences towards racial discrimination and fraud associated with the church and the event were publicized in numerous media outlets … which injured Pastor Doyle's reputation, that of his church, and the reputation of Mr. Clark as well." They further allege that James made no reasonable inquiry to verify her alleged concerns and that, had she done so, she would have learned "about the generous history of Cornerstone Church, the upstanding reputation of both Plaintiffs, and the fact that the Reawaken America Tour had not a single publicized act of violence at any of their events, nor has the tour been legitimately accused of any crimes, illicit behavior, or the violation of any laws or ordinances." Plaintiffs also allege that they had "reached out to law enforcement and local government officials to ensure the safety of the event and that all local rules, laws, and ordinances would be followed."

Plaintiffs further allege that no ReAwaken America event has ever been associated with the racial theories or violence with which James's letter purported to connect them, and her statements were "negligent, reckless and a dereliction of her duty to exercise due diligence in her representation as Attorney General and in doing so, is an abuse of power."

Plaintiffs allege that James's politically motivated letter created a "smear" on their names and damaged their community relationships. The publicity surrounding James's letter resulted in "numerous cancellations of ticket sales by patrons who originally planned to attend the event prior to hearing the derisive false reports."

Finally, plaintiffs allege that, as a result, they were forced to hire additional security to ensure the safety of the participants and attendees. Ultimately, the event "went off without a single issue." …

The court held that plaintiffs had adequately alleged the letter was unconstitutionally coercive:

To state a claim that the government violated the First Amendment through coercion, "a plaintiff must plausibly allege conduct that, viewed in context, could be reasonably understood to convey a threat of adverse government action in order to punish or suppress the plaintiff's speech." …

Here, after linking plaintiffs and the event's speakers to racist theories and past incidents of racist violence, defendant listed various state anti-discrimination laws and their penalties, concluding that her office "stand[s] ready to investigate any violation of the laws above and, if necessary, to enforce them to the fullest extent available." Plaintiffs allege that they perceived this letter as a thinly veiled threat meant to cause them to cancel the event, accompanied by the specter of prosecution.

As this Court stated in its prior RR&O [Report, Recommendations & Order]:

Here, defendant's letter has a heavy-handed, ominous tone and tells plaintiffs that: (1) she considers their own past statements concerning and "extremist," (2) she believes that "such rhetoric" could lead to "violent or unlawful conduct" at the upcoming event; and (3) she "stands ready" to investigate and enforce violations of New York laws, whose penalties she enumerates.

Such statements might reasonably be understood as threats as opposed to a government official's speech that is "nothing more than a well-reasoned and sincere entreaty in support of his own political perspective." …

[D]efendant's assertion that her letter can only be construed to convey a request to avoid or prevent unlawful conduct is unavailing at the pleading stage. In NRA v. Vullo (2024), the Supreme Court rejected such an argument:

For the same reasons, this Court cannot simply credit Vullo's assertion that "pursuing conceded violations of the law …is an obvious alternative explanation" for her actions that defeats the plausibility of any coercive threat raising First Amendment concerns… Of course, discovery in this case might show that the allegations of coercion are false, or that certain actions should be understood differently in light of newly disclosed evidence. At this stage, though, the Court must assume the well-pleaded factual allegations in the complaint are true.

Given this context, the fact that defendant's letter did not cause plaintiffs to cancel their event does not preclude a finding that defendant's letter was an unlawful attempt to coerce them to do so. See Okwedy v. Molinari (2d Cir. 2003) ("A public-official defendant who threatens to employ coercive state power to stifle protected speech violates a plaintiff's First Amendment rights.")…. Therefore, construing the allegations of the complaint in plaintiffs' favor, and considering the above authorities, the Court concludes that plaintiffs have plausibly alleged that they "reasonably understood" defendant's letter to be an attempt to suppress the exercise of their First Amendment rights. Bantam Books, Inc. v. Sullivan (1963).

The court also held that plaintiffs had adequately alleged the letter was unconstitutionally retaliatory:

"Where a private plaintiff sues public officials, the Second Circuit states the elements [of a retaliation claim] as follows: (1) Plaintiffs have a right protected by the First Amendment; (2) Defendants' actions were motivated or substantially caused by Plaintiffs' exercise of that right; and (3) Defendants' actions caused Plaintiffs some injury." … [D]efendant does not contest that plaintiffs satisfy the first element of a retaliation claim…. [And] plaintiffs' complaint alleges facts which, construed as true, would allow a reasonable person to infer that defendant was motivated to send her letter due to the political content of plaintiffs' speech ….

[D]efendant argues that plaintiffs fail to adequately plead that defendant's actions caused them legally cognizable harm. The Court discussed the issue of harm in its prior RR&O in the context of standing, noting that "[v]arious non-speech related harms are sufficient to give a plaintiff standing" for purposes of a First Amendment retaliation claim. In particular, the Court reviewed authority holding that "reputational harms" may constitute such an injury. The Court observed:

Here, plaintiffs' complaint is replete with allegations that defendant's letter falsely associated them with racist, "white nationalist ideals;" the "Great Replacement Theory;" a racially motivated shooting in Buffalo, New York; and other "racially motivated violence." Plaintiffs allege specific damages flowing from defendant's statements: injury to plaintiffs' reputations; harm to Doyle's community relationships, and specifically with communities of color; and an increased likelihood of violence towards plaintiffs….

Thus, construing the above allegations in plaintiffs' favor, the Court concludes that they have sufficiently alleged injury for purposes of their retaliation claim….

The magistrate judge also concluded that the law was sufficiently well-established that it recommended denying qualified immunity to the defendants. An excerpt:

[I]t has long been clearly established that "implied threats to employ coercive state power to stifle protected speech" violate the First Amendment. Hammerhead Enters., Inc. v. Brezenoff (2d Cir. 1983).

In 2003, the Court of Appeals reversed the dismissal of a complaint filed by plaintiffs against the Borough President of Staten Island who sent a letter to a billboard company with whom plaintiffs had contracted to produce and display billboards conveying plaintiffs' beliefs that, under biblical teachings, homosexuality is a sin. Okwedy v. Molinari (2d Cir. 2003). The letter stated that defendant and many community members found the billboards offensive, noting that the company "derives substantial economic benefits" from billboards on Staten Island and asking the company to contact defendant's legal counsel to discuss the matter. The billboard company took the signs down the same day.

The Court of Appeals held that plaintiffs had adequately pled a First Amendment claim; that a jury could find that the defendant's letter contained "an implicit threat of retaliation;" and that a "public-official defendant who threatens to employ coercive state power to stifle protected speech violates a plaintiff's First Amendment rights." …

And the court held that plaintiffs had adequately alleged defamation, despite defendant's claim of absolute privilege:

"… [A]n executive official is absolutely privileged to publish false and defamatory matter of another in the exercise of his executive function if the matter has some relation to the executive proceeding in which the official is acting." … In determining whether absolute privilege applies, the court considers two factors: (1) the personal position or status of the speaker, and (2) the subject matter of the statement and the forum in which it is made in light of the speaker's public duties. Under the second prong, it is not sufficient that the subject matter of defendant's speech merely "relate to" his public duties. Rather, the speech must be made "during his performance of an essential part of his public duties."

Under this authority, defendant is not entitled to dismissal of plaintiffs' defamation claims based on absolute immunity. The general duties of the New York Attorney General are set forth in New York Executive Law § 63. As relevant here, they include: the prosecution and defense of all proceedings in which the state is interested; the investigation, upon the request of the governor or other state officials, of certain indictable offenses; upon the request of the commissioner of labor or the state division of human rights, the prosecution of civil actions for discrimination; the prosecution of criminal offenses; and application to state courts for orders enjoining fraudulent or illegal acts.

In her current papers, defendant also cites N.Y. Exec. Law § 79-n(3), which provides that the attorney general may apply in the name of the state to a court to enjoin unlawful bias-related violence of intimidation. She also cites a penalty provision of an equal rights law that states that notice of any action under that section must be served on the attorney general.

However, the letter to plaintiffs was not written to fulfill any of these duties because defendant had initiated no investigation, prosecution, or any other governmental process against plaintiffs. In fact, defendant does not argue that plaintiffs had engaged in any actual or suspected acts that would fall within the scope of her law-enforcement authority…. Absent some factual predicate for a government official to believe that a citizen may have engaged in conduct that warrants the scrutiny of his or her office, that official is performing no duties that the doctrine of absolute privilege is meant to protect….

Tricia Sophia Lindsay represents plaintiff.

Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law. Naturally, his posts here (like the opinions of the other bloggers) are his own, and not endorsed by any institution. He is also the co-host of the Free Speech Unmuted podcast.

Apple’s grey list: Words and phrases your iPhone won’t help you spell

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There are more than 100,000 words and phrases that the tech giant has restricted its predictive text from completing, research shows

“Australia is a very bad place to question your sexuality on MDMA with NSA spies” might be a text you would wish to send to your friends, if you have had an extraordinarily complicated night out in Sydney.

But your iPhone won’t help you spell any of it if you try to swipe-type it – that is, by gliding a finger across a keyboard as opposed to pecking out a word.

New research shows that Apple has a “grey list” of more than 100,000 words and phrases that it has restricted from being completed by its predictive text.

So if a user wishes to type phrases such as “Dictator Trump”, “NSA spies” or, surprisingly, “Australia is a very bad place”, “question your sexuality” or “on MDMA”, they will have to spell them out.

Apple’s predictive text will not suggest these words. And, if a user attempts to swipe-type them, they will often stumble, changing “United Ireland” to “United Iceland”, for example, or “Tibetan independence” to “Tibetan independences”.

The “grey list” was discovered by the independent researcher Derik Kauffman, whose other projects include using AI to find errors in scientific papers. After noticing that he had problems typing certain phrases – but not others – on his iPhone, Kauffman was able to unearth a table containing the list, encoded in Apple’s operating system.

The list he found includes some unsurprising words and phrases. Apple’s iPhone will not autocomplete references to drugs, sexual acts and, perhaps in the spirit of corporate protection, “iMessage isn’t working”.

But it also includes possibly politically charged phrases, which Kauffman described to Reuters as a “light-touch form of censorship”. Among these are “Trayvon Martin”, the name of the Black teenager shot in Florida in 2012, “Kashmir dispute”, “Muslim extremist” and “male chauvinist pig”.

In Hebrew, Apple will not autocomplete the phrase “the occupied territories”. In Arabic, it will not autocomplete the phrase “the Hamas movement”.

The list is one more glimpse into the dark bowels of how autocorrect – the chirpy keyboard partner that helps you look more educated to your loved ones – actually works.

The technology has taken many forms. In its earliest version, Unix, an operating system, would look up each word a user typed against a table of words, to determine if this word was spelled correctly.

This evolved into n-grams, a tool which allows an operating system to statistically predict what words might come next, based on the words before it. These form the basis of Apple’s familiar autocorrect, which was known to change expletives into prudish alternatives: “fork” and “ducking”, among other gaffes.

Last year, Apple launched a new autocorrect – a “transformer-based” system that was more akin to an LLM. Reddit sleuths saw this as a possible reason behind a spate of avant-garde spelling suggestions their iPhones made last year.

This hard-coded list illuminates one longstanding mystery behind the world’s spelling coach. Others remain, such as a brief glitch in which users of the dictation feature saw the name “Trump” flash across their screen when they spoke the word “racist” into their iPhones – which Apple quickly remedied.

Apple said that it designed its keyboard to avoid offering inappropriate or offensive predictive suggestions. It said iPhone users could always type out specific words or phrases, and override predictive text suggestions to achieve their desired meaning.

Amazon’s AI shopping agent moves into text messages, taking orders and tracking prices

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 6, 2026 at 8:07 amNovember 6, 2026 at 9:35 am

Amazon is rolling out a new feature to let customers shop by text message — sending requests to its Alexa for Shopping AI agent, which will search for products, compare options and place orders on their behalf.

The idea is to make shopping work more like messaging a personal assistant. Amazon says the agent works in the background after it gets a request or a photo, and texts back when the task is done.

It’s part throwback and part hot new thing, reflecting a broader shift in how tech companies are offering AI agents for everyday use. OpenAI added texting to Dot, its personal AI agent, last month, and DoorDash rolled out text ordering in September.

The expansion of Amazon’s AI shopping assistant comes amid a larger fight over who controls online shopping when agents do the buying. Amazon has moved to block outside agents from Meta, Perplexity, Google and OpenAI from shopping its site while building its own.

The text thread is also a new place for Amazon to sell. The company says order updates, delivery notifications and deals will eventually land in the same conversation.

Digiday reports that sponsored products can appear among the options the agent texts back, free to advertisers for now, while purchases the agent completes on its own stay ad-free. Amazon reported $68.6 billion in advertising sales in 2025.

The text feature started rolling out Friday to a small group of U.S. customers by invitation, and millions more will get access in the coming weeks, the company says.

Customers who opt in get a contact called “Alexa Shopping” and can send it requests that range from a note that they’re out of dog food to a photo of a recipe they want turned into a grocery order, according to Amazon’s examples.

The agent searches, texts back with options and completes the purchase with the customer’s approval. Customers can also turn on auto-buy to have it purchase an item automatically when the price hits their target. It can also watch for price drops, reorder past purchases and check on packages, and Amazon says a conversation started by text will carry over to the Amazon app, the website and Alexa devices.

Amazon launched Alexa for Shopping in May, merging its Rufus shopping chatbot with Alexa+, the generative AI version of its voice assistant.

Let there be LEDs: Tech upgrade helps James Turrell’s Skyspace shine again at UW art museum

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Geek Life: Fun stories, memes, humor and other random items at the intersection of tech, science, business and culture.  SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 6, 2026 at 8:01 amNovember 6, 2026 at 9:42 am

For most of the past dozen years, a James Turrell artwork on the University of Washington campus in Seattle — built to glow at sunrise and sunset — has been running on daylight alone.

That changes Nov. 14, when UW’s Henry Art Gallery relights “Light Reign,” its Turrell Skyspace, after a restoration that replaced aging lights with hundreds of new fixtures and a control system synced to the sun.

Originally installed in 2003 near the Henry’s entrance along 15th Avenue N.E., “Light Reign” is one of dozens of skyspaces by Turrell, the 83-year-old artist known for turning light itself into his medium.

Inside the artwork’s oval chamber, LEDs bathe the walls in slowly shifting color, and when the weather cooperates, a retractable lid opens to an oculus that frames the sky. From outside, the glass-paneled shell lights up with its own program — a 21st-century mood ring just steps from a bronze George Washington who has been gazing west toward the Olympics since 1909.

The relighting comes as the Henry, Washington’s first public art museum, heads into its 100th year. The museum opened on campus in February 1927, and officials are hinting that bigger changes are on the way for the centennial.

Trouble with “Light Reign” started around 2015, said Sage Sommer, the Henry’s director of exhibitions and collections. The original lights, by then more than a decade old, were reaching the end of their lifespan, and over time the museum lost the ability to run them.

Through those years, the Skyspace stayed open. Visitors could still sit inside, meditate and watch clouds and birds pass over the oculus, just without the color.

After years of planning, the Henry settled on a full lighting overhaul that also folds in ideas Turrell has used in his newer skyspaces. The exterior already ran on LEDs, but a very early generation of them, while the interior and oculus were lit by neon tubes made by Western Neon.

The museum didn’t work with Turrell directly. Instead it worked with lighting designer Ryan Pike, whose company, RHMP, works only on Turrell installations, from upgrades of existing pieces to new projects. Pike has worked on Turrell artworks since 2013.

For Pike, the toughest part was the exterior. “It’s just over 700 lights, in nine DMX universes, so each fixture can output independently of the rest,” he said in an email. DMX is the standard protocol for controlling stage and architectural lighting. Splitting the facade across multiple universes lets every fixture be programmed on its own.

Sommer said the Skyspace isn’t strictly time-based media, a museum term for art that relies on equipment and unfolds over time. But it raises the same questions. Sometimes the fix is simple, like converting a VHS tape to a digital file. Other times the hardware is integral to the work. Some artists insist a piece be shown only on a CRT TV, and some come back with a remastered version and want only that one shown.

“We work really closely with the artists to make sure that it is as they envision,” she said.

The Henry isn’t the only museum to face the problem. In 2023, Minneapolis’ Walker Art Center restored its own Turrell skyspace, “Sky Pesher,” after it became impossible to find the cold cathode tubes — a 1920s-era technology — that powered its sunrise and sunset lighting program.

Working with Turrell and his studio, the Walker switched to LEDs, which allowed more control and a much wider range of color. What had been “shades of tinted white” is now multicolored, Joe King, the Walker’s director of collections and exhibition management, said in an interview published by the museum.

The Henry’s new system uses roughly 840 LED fixtures, about 710 outside and 130 inside, Sommer said. The exterior program kicks in a half-hour before dawn and dusk. The morning show is brief, about 36 minutes, while the evening one currently runs about four hours after sunset.

“The very cool thing about this space, because we are so far north, is just how connected to sunrise and sunset it is, and how drastically different it is throughout the year,” Sommer said.

The roof has its own engineering pedigree. The Skyspace’s hydraulic lid, which lifts and slides out of the way, was designed by submarine manufacturers in Everett. It stays shut in the rain.

The museum closed the Skyspace in late July and used the downtime for physical restoration, too: repainting the interior, refinishing the teak benches, waxing floors, replacing worn locks, refitting doors that had swollen and cleaning out the gutters. Everything had to be rewired, and the project took longer than expected.

“Light Reign” is the most visible piece in the Henry’s collection of about 30,000 objects, including more than 18,000 textiles and 7,000 works on paper. Only a sliver is ever on view, so the Henry is putting its collection database online. The project was funded by UW’s Student Technology Fee. Anyone can book an appointment at its study center to see a piece up close.

Technology is also the focus of the Henry’s centennial exhibition, opening in late February. Swagato Chakravorty, associate curator, said he was surprised that no major museum had mounted a large, ambitious show on contemporary art and technology in decades, even as Seattle was transformed around it. His show looks at technology “from below,” focusing on the people and communities most directly affected rather than a “great man” story.

The exhibition is loosely organized around three threads. One is bioethics, including a work the Henry commissioned in 2002 with UW geneticist Mary-Claire King, which is being revived 25 years later. Another pairs Indigenous knowledge with tools like 3D printing and algorithms. The third is technology’s material toll, from data centers to water use.

Chakravorty said he deliberately avoids taking a side on whether technology is good or bad for art.

“Writing is a technology. Cave painting was a technology,” he said. As for AI: “It’s not going to kill art.”

The public unveiling of the refurbished “Light Reign” runs 3:30 to 6 p.m. Nov. 14, with remarks from Henry Executive Director Kris Lewis and the exterior illumination starting at 4 p.m. Admission is free, though the Henry encourages RSVPs, and interior viewing is limited to 25 people at a time for about 10 minutes each.

Bitcoin ETFs could surpass mutual funds in AUM by 2028: Bloomberg analyst

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

As we are about to enter the third year of the Spot Bitcoin [BTC] ETF being launched, it appears that BTC ETFs have a significantly stronger influence on BTC price returns than corporate Bitcoin accumulation.

According to the latest analysis by Ecoinometrics, an inflow of 10,000 BTC into spot Bitcoin ETFs corresponds to an estimated 3.2% boost in monthly Bitcoin returns. In comparison, the same amount of Bitcoin purchased by public companies contributes approximately 0.7% to monthly returns.

This suggests that ETF purchases have roughly 4.5 times the impact of corporate buying on Bitcoin’s price performance.

Adding more to this analysis, Bloomberg’s James Seyffart suggests that ETFs could surpass mutual funds in total Assets Under Management (AUM) within the next two to three years.

Their growth is only accelerating. If we stay on the same trend we’ve been on since the summer it will happen in ~two years.

Confirming his prediction, Syeffart used a chart and highlighted that ETFs accounted for 40.21% of total fund assets as of September 2026. And given the recent growth trends, ETFs could reach the 50% mark as early as September 2028 if the three-month growth rate continues.

However, other projections estimate this milestone between November 2028 and December 2029, depending on the period considered.

At the same time, ETFs rolling 12-month growth rate have also reached 3.74% in September 2026, above the historical average of 2.78% since the 2020 COVID market bottom. This indicates that ETF adoption is expanding faster than its long-term average.

This comes as BTC ETFs are in their outflows streak. According to Farisde Investors data, from 1st to 5th November 2026, Bitcoin ETFs saw mostly outflows. In fact, so far in November, it has already recorded $869.82 million in outflows. The only exception being on the 4th of November, when the BTC ETFs recorded a net inflow of $74.6 million.

All of this happened as the price of BTC was trading at $76,102.46 after a drop of 52% in the past 24 hours.

That said, besides Bitcoin ETFs, Ethereum [ETH] ETFs also recorded $272.09  in outflows, and Binance [BNB] ETFs recorded $271.01K in outflows. Meanwhile, XRP ETFs were funded by $529.51K in outflows, Solana [SOL] ETFs had $1.18M in outflows, and Hyperliquid [HYPE] ETFs also saw $7.66 million in outflows.

In short, the pattern suggests risk-off sentiment across crypto at the start of November, and not just in Bitcoin. So, given the current data, it remains to be seen whether Seyffart’s predictions turn into reality or not.

Claret & Hugh can reveal today that, despite reports to the contrary, David Sullivan had no input into the decision to appoint Scott Parker as West Ham United’s new manager.

Some reports in the national media have suggested that Sullivan reacted to the Hammers’ back-to-back defeats against Southampton and West Bromwich Albion by convincing the board to sack Nuno Espírito Santo and appoint Parker. However, information received by C&H indicates that this was not the case.

Instead, we understand that the decision was driven by West Ham chief executive Karim Virani, who, following meetings on Monday, approached the board for permission to dismiss Nuno and appoint Parker. The former Hammers midfielder was already the preferred candidate, with one club insider describing him to us today as “the perfect fit”.

And it has very much been Virani liaising with Daniel Kretinsky which has prompted the club to change direction so quickly.

Karim Virani was the driving force behind the appointment of Scott Parker

The concern surrounding Nuno’s position had been building for some time and was not simply a reaction to two disappointing results. There was growing dissatisfaction within the club over performances across a number of matches, with the defeat to West Bromwich Albion effectively proving to be the last straw.

Virani subsequently decided to act more quickly than originally anticipated, with performances showing no sign of improvement. As Claret & Hugh revealed earlier this week, Monday’s meeting brought forward a dismissal which had initially been expected to come after further consideration.

Parker was very much at the top of the list, and Virani sought the board’s approval to proceed with the change. Our latest information makes an important distinction between the board approving the appointment and Sullivan personally driving the decision.

Far from being a Sullivan-led intervention following the Southampton and West Brom defeats, C&H understands that it was Virani who took the initiative to end Nuno’s tenure and bring Parker back to West Ham.

Cristiano Ronaldo: 9 of his international records that will never be broken

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Cristiano Ronaldo has called time on his incredible international career with Portugal and will leave behind a legacy hard to match.

Ronaldo is bowing out from international duty after 234 caps and 146 goals for Portugal. He won the Euros with them in 2016 and went on to triumph twice in the UEFA Nations League.

A World Cup evaded him, but Ronaldo made his mark at that competition too over the years, scoring 11 times across six tournaments.

As one of the best players in history, his efforts in a Portugal shirt will stand the test of time in the record books.

He boasts plenty of records in international football – and these ones might never be beaten.

There are only three men with more than 100 international goals, but Ronaldo is way out in front. He quits 20 ahead of Lionel Messi, who also retired from international football this year, and 38 ahead of Ali Daei.

The active footballer closest to Ronaldo’s record is Romelu Lukaku on 94, but at the age of 33, it’s hard to see him catching up. The same goes for 38-year-old Robert Lewandowski on 92.

We’re pretty sure Harry Kane has plenty more goals for England in the tank, but enough to overtake Ronaldo? He’d need more than 50, so it’s a long shot.

If anyone stands half a chance it’s probably Erling Haaland on 65 goals, but his chances might depend on how many major tournaments Norway qualify for. It’s easy to forget the 2026 World Cup was their first major tournament since Euro 2000.

There’s Kylian Mbappe on 67 for France as well, but it makes you realise how impressive Ronaldo’s record is when you consider he’s not even halfway to reaching it.

Ronaldo has been able to reach those lofty goalscoring heights by playing more than anyone else in the international game.

His 234 caps for Portugal are 26 more than the amount Messi managed for Argentina. Third on the list is the still-active Luka Modric, 28 behind his former Real Madrid teammate but nearing the end at the age of 41.

Qatar’s Hassan Al-Haydos is the next nearest active challenger on 193 caps. The Al Sadd captain is only 35, so might end up not too far off, but it’ll still be a tall order.

Ronaldo played for his country for 23 years, after all, and that longevity is going to be hard for anyone to match.

Talking of longevity, Ronaldo hasn’t been wasting time at the tail end of his career. He actually scored nearly twice as many goals for Portugal after turning 30 than before.

It probably makes sense, with Ronaldo playing more centrally as the years went on after starting out as a winger.

In total, he scored 94 goals for Portugal after turning 30, compared to 52 before.

Unsurprisingly, it’s Messi behind him in the ranking, with an impressive 68 goals for Argentina after his 30th birthday.

But Ronaldo’s record will take some beating. Only four other players have ever scored 94 international goals, let alone after the age of 30. This one’s probably sticking.

Similar principle here. Ronaldo scored in 96 of the Portugal games he played in. Only he, Messi and Daei have reached that amount of goals in international football regardless of whether they were scoring braces or hat-tricks.

Ronaldo scored against a grand total of 49 different countries for Portugal, meaning he had three more victims on the international stage than Messi.

An ever-expanding World Cup might help, but it’s hard to imagine anyone scoring against 50 different countries.

It’d probably have to be someone from Europe or Africa, since those confederations have the most members. But they’d still be relying on the luck of the draw in qualifiers for tournaments.

Ronaldo scored for Portugal in 23 different years, which is longer than most players’ international careers – let alone for them to be consistently delivering in front of goal.

Even if a player was to make their international debut at 18, they’d have to play into their forties and score without fail every year to surpass Ronaldo’s record.

Ronaldo might have missed out on the all-time World Cup scoring record, but he does boast the most goals at European Championships.

His final tally of 14 is five higher than the next best, Michel Platini on nine.

Alvaro Morata and Kane have seven each, but will be 35 and 34 by the time the next Euros start. Kane could give him a run for his money, but he might only have one more shot at it. If the next Euros are his last, he’d need to score eight times to beat Ronaldo’s record – something only Platini has done at a single Euros tournament before.

Ronaldo is one of only two outfield players to make more than 20 Euros appearances, so if anyone wants to threaten his goalscoring record, their ratio is going to have to be high.

As stated, Ronaldo played in 30 Euros matches. It only takes seven games to go from the group stage all the way to the final, so someone would have to do that four times and more to beat his record.

UEFA have previously put plans to expand the Euros on the backburner, so someone would need to have a near-20-year international career – and do well in it – to come close to Ronaldo’s record.

Ronaldo and Messi are the only outfield players to go to six World Cups – and Ronaldo scored at every one he went to.

Unless the World Cup becomes a more frequent event than one that happens every four years, someone would have to play for two decades, always qualify for the World Cup and always score at it to match Ronaldo’s record, let alone beat it.

Arsenal are champions of the autumn, and winter could prove to be their wonderland

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Title drama isn’t guaranteed every season. See Manchester City 2017/18 and Liverpool 2019/20 for a procession of one.

Nevertheless, there are usually a few honeymoon months for broadcasters and fans to dream of a proper footrace before the strongest on the savannah hunt down the antelopes. Super Sunday has to have meaning, right?

Jose Mourinho claimed that Chelsea were “champions of the autumn” when his side defeated Liverpool to sit top of the tree just before the November international break in 2014. The Blues had only dropped four points in 11 matches. The Portuguese said: “I think we have a very good chance to win the title, but I think we, Arsenal, Manchester City, Manchester United and Southampton are all still in the race.” Southampton. Hmmm.

Jose was bluffing, bigging up rivals who weren’t in the same ballpark. His side cantered over the line.

Man City and Arsenal were the closest challengers in that campaign, losing seven matches to Chelsea’s three. Manchester United were in a post-Ferguson funk, and Brendan Rodgers was experiencing the last rites as Anfield manager.

The 2025/26 class has a similar issue in the quality of challengers. Enzo Maresca’s New Model Army blasted out of the blocks with seven straight wins but have been hobbled by Pep’s familiar foes in Brighton and Aston Villa. It may be that the looming prospect of losing points through other means is beginning to sink deeper into their psyche.

Those 114 charges aren’t so much the elephant in the room as the rat in the kitchen. Arsenal are dining for one right now. They are master chefs while the rest are still trying to find the ingredients to lay an oven glove at the top table.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Liverpool’s loss to the champions last Sunday appeared to deflate the Anfield air and any sense that the Reds might be pretenders to the throne. Truth be told, no one had Andoni Iraola’s expensively imbalanced squad as a serious contender after the problematic 2025/26 season. They didn't have a shot on target and fell nine points behind the leaders. Just one look at Jamie Carragher's face told the story.

Chelsea, meanwhile, took the lead at the Emirates, but haven’t really clicked into anything resembling a stride - at least not against title-contender opposition.

The games that Arsenal were drawing in 2024/25 - and to a lesser extent last season - are now being won. There is a seismic gap between the sheer efficiency of the Gunners and the rest of the pack. That efficiency now has extra security and titanium screws in all areas barring the forward line. The midfield monster that is Bruno Guimaraes roars whenever a tackle is made. Ezri Konsa has slotted in as if he knew William Saliba’s every move. Missing Bukayo Saka? It’s not a big deal anymore.

Arsenal know who they are and are expanding their limited attacking horizons without losing that deadlock bolt at the back. The lack of a striker isn’t a problem when Gabriel and Konsa can win a game at both ends. The North London team have been victorious in 14 out of their last 15 Premier League matches .

While Xabi Alonso and Iraola are trying to restore order, Mikel Arteta’s squad are in absolute lockstep.

During that hard-fought victory over Sunderland in September, a game that Arsenal would surely have drawn in previous incarnations, Carragher mooted that Arteta was trying to become the new Sir Alex of the Premier League. The Spaniard hasn't quite got the caustic asides, dry wit or trophy haul of the legendary Scot, but the war machine is heavily stocked. Nothing escapes Arteta’s mind map if it means marginal gains. He is controlling the Wild West. “I have a massive storage of images.” Quite.

The Premier League will celebrate its 35th champion next year and, to be brutally honest, it is searching for its reinvention as an entertainment package. The coalface clashes between highly decorated clubs have felt lukewarm for a while now, like gentle sparring from former greats on a senior tour.

Squeaky bum time in May seems fanciful. Arteta has learned that the W column is all that counts. When Hull City turn up at the Emirates without a win in seven, the Gunners will look to hit the 27-point mark, one behind Jose’s highly drilled warriors 12 years ago. "I know that the negative moment will arrive, the defeat will arrive, and we are prepared for that,” said Mourinho.

It could be a long winter for the chasing pack when that late November to March cycle restarts after the Nations League. Arsenal 2.0 are bulletproof.

Aluminium canopy encases waterfront pavilion in Providence by Architecture Research Office

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Steel columns support the expansive canopy of this green-roofed pavilion at 195 District Park in Providence, Rhode Island, which has been completed by New York-based studio Architecture Research Office.

Conceived by the studio as a “year-round amenity” for the public, the 195 District Park Pavilion accommodates two local businesses, an indoor-outdoor dining area and public restrooms overlooking the Providence River.

The pavilion’s position at the park’s highest point led Architecture Research Office (ARO) to design its multifaceted structure as a “beacon” that would be accessible from multiple entrances.

“Centrally located within 195 District Park, the pavilion is a park amenity that welcomes and orients visitors while supporting park programming,” studio principal Kim Yao told Dezeen.

“The pavilion is located at the highest point within the park in response to the floodplain of the adjacent Providence River.”

“This positioning created an opportunity for the building to serve as a beacon, visible from multiple entry points to the park,” Yao added.

Fronted by a plaza, the pavilion is composed of a metal frame that has been cloaked with cedar wood in a nod to the area’s local architecture.

Its structure is wrapped by an expansive aluminium canopy, which is supported by a series of steel columns and extends over the plaza to shelter an outdoor seating space.

Atop the structure, the sloped green roof helps to blend the pavilion into its surrounding landscape, while also limiting stormwater run-off and screening the rooftop’s mechanical systems.

“As a year-round amenity, the pavilion opens to shaded exterior areas in the summer while providing shelter during colder seasons,” Yao said.

“It also provides occupants with views of the river and surrounding areas,” she added.

Inside, the 3,500-square-foot building opens up to the indoor seating area, complete with exposed wooden joists.

Flanking the space are the pavilion’s operable storefront doors, which open up to the adjacent plaza and enable passive ventilation throughout the space.

Additional facilities include public restrooms located at the pavilion’s southern corner.

Its structure is perched beside a curved pedestrian bridge completed by Inform Studio and Buro Happold in 2020 that stretches over the Providence River to link two parks on the east and west sides of the city’s riverfront.

Other pavilions recently featured on Dezeen include a temporary marketing space in São Paulo built from mass-timber scrap and a basket-like aluminium pavilion in New York.

Architects: Architecture Research OfficeLandscape architect: Agency Landscape + PlanningStructural engineer: Hatfield GroupMEP engineer: ColladoLighting designer: Lighting WorkshopCivil engineer: Fuss & O’NeillGeotechnical engineer: RMA EnvironmentalPlacemaking and food service: AgoraOwner’s representative: ColliersConstruction manager: Shawmut Design and Construction

Micro-Investing App Fees – $4 a Month Costs 9.6% a Year on $500

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

To see what a micro-investing app’s flat monthly fee costs you, multiply it by 12 and divide by your balance. On a small balance, that yearly percentage can be close to or above the 10% a year large U.S. stocks have averaged since 1928. Until it falls under 1%, invest through a broker with no account fee.

Acorns charges $4, $8, or $12 a month, depending on the plan, and Stash’s plan costs $12 a month, or $108 for a year paid up front. Those prices stay the same whatever your balance, so they take the biggest percentage of a small one.

At $4 a month, you pay $48 a year, which is 9.6% of a $500 balance. That 9.6% is nearly all of the 10% a year the S&P 500, an index of 500 large U.S. companies, has returned on average since 1928, according to data from NYU professor Aswath Damodaran.

Index stock mutual funds, which track an index like the S&P 500, charged an average fee of 0.05% a year in 2025, the Investment Company Institute found. On the same $500, that’s 25 cents a year against the app’s $48.

When you do the math, count every balance the fee covers, the app’s retirement account included, not just its regular investing account. A $12 monthly fee on $2,000 across both is $144 a year, or 7.2%.

Round-ups, which invest the spare change from each purchase, can be worth the fee while they’re the only way you’d invest at all. You can build the same habit for free, though. Check what your round-ups added last month, then set up an automatic monthly investment of that amount at an online broker with no account fee. Pick one that sells fractional shares, so even $20 buys a slice of a fund that tracks the S&P 500.

The fee drops below 1% a year, a tenth of the S&P 500’s 10% average, once your balance reaches 1,200 times the monthly fee. That’s a balance of $4,800 on a $4 plan and $14,400 on a $12 plan.

To set up that automatic investment, compare the best online brokers.

Review: Titanium Court (Switch 2) - The Freakiest Dream You've Ever Had

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It sounds like the stuff of dreams. Fever dreams. The theatre audience is waiting, and you’re in the wings. Without warning, an MC announces that the lead actress is unavailable, so you’re thrust onstage as understudy – but without a clue what you’re supposed to do. A solicitor’s billboard.

Titanium Court, the creation of New York-based developer AP Thomson, is driven by a singular vision. It mashes up almost anything it can get its hands on – whether it’s different game genres, different musical styles, or just different images of sports or snappy tailoring or the Highway Code. Since the only common thread seems to be the developer’s mind, it feels strangely personal despite the miscellaneous compilation of ideas jumping out of nowhere. A man in a suit playing a trumpet.

Looking at screenshots, you’ll think this is a match-three game – and it sort of is: it’s a strategic, roguelike, narrative-driven match-three game. In fact, the match-three game seems as surprised to be there as you are. Having been dropped into the theatrical setting, you soon establish that your role in the drama is that of the faerie queen, ruling (cluelessly at first) over your royal court. Said court sits in a terrain of matchable tiles that can each be swapped with adjacent ones to make three in a row. Matched tiles disappear and anything sitting above them falls down the screen, with new tiles appearing from the top to fill any gaps. Depending on what you match, you will receive resources of different kinds – food, stone, wood or water. As you make moves, a bar depletes, freezing the terrain once it runs out. A pair of sunglasses.

That whole match-three-ing phase is called 'high tide'. Once 'low tide' arrives, the court goes to war. The terrain you’ve done your best to design becomes the battlefield. Usually, there are enemy fortifications on it, and you must spend your resources to be ready to deal with them by training troops or workers or setting up defences. However, this is all part of a larger campaign, so resources should be strategically preserved for subsequent rounds. A basketball swishing the net.

Between runs, you are returned to your faerie court, where mystery unfolds to an eerily wistful tune. These sections are presented in a top-down view of the royal residence, with icons to select your actions: visit the dining hall, talk to your aide, take a shower, etc. The story is told here by the characters in the play — or perhaps the cast — always keeping logic just about at arm’s length. The repetition of the high-and-low-tide gather-and-hunt routine is consistently fun, but it’s the in-betweens that provide pacing, a narrative arc, and an opportunity to put the game down until your next chapter, breaking up the addictive loops common to match-threes. A rock star smashing their guitar.

Through all of this, it’s not clear whether your wide-eyed confusion is part of your role. Are the other characters helping you according to the script, or are the actors out of character, helping you to understand the script? But the script is unfinished anyway, the MC announces up front: the writers are racing you to the ending. Even your tooltip is in on the whole charade, personified in dialogue with you. The overall effect is something bizarrely coherent. It’s a jumble of a concept but, wrapped in the framing device of the theatre, nothing can technically be out of place. A 'No Parking' sign.

If we try and strip it all down to the gameplay essentials, Titanium Court has a moreish tension going on. The strategising grows in sophistication the more you play, and the goals of the game get clearer. However, those goals always lie on the other side of the randomness of the tile-matching mechanics. The balance of skill and luck is just about right, and complemented by the interspersed court segments. Success feels like your own achievement, while failure — although sometimes due to cruel tile-generation — affords an opportunity to relax in the court and explore its eccentricities – or who knows what might happen, frankly? A sticking-plaster.

So there’s a solid game under all the theatrics, but the theatrics are what will grab and hold your attention. The writing is thoughtful without pontificating, funny without leaning on jokes, and adventurous without losing sight of its absurdity. Perhaps the most absurd thing is how naturally this gels with a genre so far removed from Titanium Court’s philosophical ambitions. You could argue that the repetitive parts of the game wear out their welcome – but that’s not before the meta game gives you an out. You can play again and again if you like, but this is a controlled story that has an opinion about how and when it should end. It wouldn’t be fair to judge it on what comes after the curtain call. A glass of wine.

The graphical style also works wonderfully. Despite the tight colour palette on a dark screen, things jump to life. The eye-catching, whimsical images that pop up will bounce or fly or slide across the window in ways that fit the action perfectly. The thrill of an out-of-nowhere, pixel-art touchdown as the battle music rocks out is just perfect. A dog catching a frisbee.

Theatrical performance, narrative adventure, strategy game, match-three… Shuffling its deck of faerie queens and love potions and slam dunks and Ray-Bans, Titanium Court is the freakiest dream you’ve ever had. It might baffle your psychoanalyst, but that’s no reason to wake up.

Why Running Slower Can Make You a Faster Runner

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Runners love to push themselves. But holding back can help create a stronger base, especially when you’re training for a big race.

Slowing down can actually help you get faster.

On this week’s episode of The Amazing Runner’s World Show, Aly and Kristine discuss why slow runs deserve a crucial spot in your training plan.

In the previous episode, we talked about how to pace the five boroughs of the NYC Marathon.

Why West Brom’s £2m summer addition is becoming a smart signing under James Morrison

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

West Brom missed their chance to move into the top two as they drew 1-1 with Southampton on Tuesday night. As they prepare to host Millwall this weekend, James Morrison‘s WBA are serious about fighting for promotion this season, winning three of their last five including an impressive 3-1 win over West Ham at the London Stadium.

The start that they have had comes off the back of a successful summer window. Under Morrison, WBA made a real effort to revamp the squad with more youth and several new additions were made with that in mind.

Jimmy-Jay Morgan has taken a lot of the plaudits after all his goalscoring exploits. However, another one of their additions is starting to break into the side, Rabby Nzingoula.

The Baggies spent a reported £2m on the 20-year-old after a decent second half of the season on loan at Nurnberg. He has been a hugely important player over the past five games where West Brom have gone unbeaten.

The 20-year-old arrived from Strasbourg with plenty of potential but relatively little experience of English football. However, three months into his Hawthorns career, he is adapting to the Championship quicker than many would have expected.

Nzingoula has gradually moved from an impact substitute to a regular part of James Morrison’s midfield, making 14 appearances and accumulating 710 minutes. Seven of those outings have been starts, while his average rating of 6.73 suggests he is providing a dependable contribution despite his young age.

There is also more to his game than simply breaking up opposition attacks. Nzingoula has recorded 41 line-breaking passes and created nine chances in his Championship minutes so far. His 1.14 chances created per 90 puts him in the 75th percentile, while his shot attempts rank in the 80th percentile.

These figures suggest West Brom have a midfielder capable of contributing further up the pitch rather than simply protecting the defence.

At 20 years old, Nzingoula has a good level of experience at decent levels. The WBA man has played 37 times in Ligue 1 as well as featuirng in Europe for Strasbourg.

His versatility is another positive. Comfortable operating as either a six or an eight, Nzingoula gives Morrison different options in central and wider midfield areas. His recent performances, including a 7.3 rating against Southampton on November 3, suggest he is beginning to find his feet.

There are still areas for improvement. He has yet to register a goal or assist, his aerial numbers are relatively modest and three yellow cards highlight the occasional risks associated with his aggressive approach.

Nevertheless, West Brom should be encouraged. Nzingoula is only 20, has already demonstrated Championship-level defensive qualities and is showing signs of becoming a more complete midfielder. For a reported £2m, the Baggies could have secured a very smart long-term investment.

I just found out you can use your Steam Wishlist to search for deals on games outside of Steam, saving my nephew more than £40 on Total War: Warhammer 3

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It earned me a bucketload of uncle points

I may be very late to the party with this discovery, but I only just realised you can connect your Steam Wishlist to a third-party deals tracker. The beauty of which is that, once hooked up, you can see what games you've been pining for are going cheap not just on Steam but through any major shopfront across the internet.

I worry that I will be coming across as 'Use this one trick to save money – greengrocers hate it!', but it's already helped save my nephew more than £40. Even if it meant using a slightly underhand method.

Two weeks ago, I got a text from my nephew asking what the best way to play Total War: Warhammer 3. When I messaged back a couple of hours later (look, I'm not a responsive uncle) to tell him it's currently £50 on Steam, but it's worth waiting because according to SteamDB's price history it's come down to £7.50 in the past, I was too late. He'd got itchy feet and bought it at full whack on Steam, using up all his birthday money.

That's when I went looking and found Is There Any Deal for the first time. (A lot of you in the comments may be throwing your hands up in despair to hear this oversight) If you've not come across it before, it's a site that tracks game sales across loads of shops, including Steam, EA Store, Epic Games Store, GamersGate, GOG. GreenManGaming, Humble. This means while a game may not be on sale on Steam you can see if it's on sale anywhere else.

In the case of Total War: Warhammer 3, while it was full price on Steam, over on the Humble Store it was discounted to £7.49. I sent a link to my nephew and then told him, using the authority only an uncle has, that it's okay to refund the game through Steam and buy the cheaper version. It's not something I've ever done myself, but he's 17 and that is a lot of money to save.

Anyway, that's recommendation the first. It was only when I was looking for a way to export my Steam Wishlist to ask RPS supporters what I should cull that I realised Is There Any Deal has another feature: you can import every game on your Wishlist to get deal notifications. (This may get a second groan of despair in the comment section.) Once linked up, the site will ping you a notification whenever games you're waiting for pop up in a deal.

Just looking at my current list, I can get Genesis Noir, Ancient Enemy, and Norco for £10 total, all of which with more than 70% discounts through stores other than Steam. So, as you can see, this could be both a saving technically and lead me to spending a lot more money than I mean to…

You may already have heard of these tools, but as I hadn't I thought it best to give them a shout out.

Now considering I've revealed myself to be a rube, what other third party tools should I be installing to weaponise my Steam Library?

How a new Pulte Florida agrihood makes conservation a buyer desire

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

PulteGroup is developing The Grow, a 1,200-acre agrihood east of Orlando planned for about 2,200 homes anchored by a 9-acre working farm. Plans leave just over half the site undeveloped, positioning the project as a growth and conservation case study.  AI Summary

For years, Central Florida’s exploding population growth and rapidly expanding suburban sprawl have clashed with local efforts to protect the region’s dwindling green space and undeveloped land. PulteGroup’s new 1,200-acre community on Orlando’s eastern edge aims to balance these seemingly contradictory interests.

The community is called The Grow. Once undeveloped, rural land, the property is set to begin morphing into a roughly 2,200-home “agrihood” centered around a 9-acre working farm. In keeping with its conservation focus, the plan calls for reserving just over half of the site from development, including wetlands, conservation areas and shared green space.

The Grow offers a potential case study in balancing growth and conservation, a tension deeply felt in Central Florida and many other high-growth Sun Belt markets. Between 2000 and 2025, the Orlando metro area’s population grew nearly 80%, from 1.64 million to 2.95 million.

That growth reshaped once-rural communities on the region’s edges, replacing undeveloped land with new subdivisions, apartments and shopping centers. Many long-time Sunshine State residents now lament the loss of “Old Florida,” marked by a landscape of open land, working ranches and orange groves that are now developed.

When Pulte, the sole homebuilder at The Grow, purchased the community’s lots in 2023, it set out to build a premier community that responded to these tensions.

“One of our biggest selling features is the openness of the community and how much greenery we left. Everywhere you look, you’re surrounded by trees,” Patrick McNamara, a Sales Manager at Pulte, told HousingWire TBD.

However, the true distinguisher between The Grow and other local communities will be its working farm, a rare amenity that carries the conservation theme beyond protecting land to putting it to use.

For Pulte, which held a grand opening for the community in late September, the farm and the extensive conservation space give the builder a way to stand out to prospective homebuyers, a crucial edge considering the challenging sales environment homebuilders are facing.

The working farm is central to the community’s agrihood concept – providing fresh, sustainably grown produce. Residents will support it by buying products, volunteering and joining educational activities.

McNamara argued that the farm creates a direct connection between residents and their food. A professional third-party company will manage the farm, but residents and families will be allowed to volunteer and then buy the finished produce at a resident-only general store that will serve as an anchor for The Grow’s commercial and retail street.

The development will include two other farms: a small demonstration farm, covering about an eighth of an acre, and a separate community farm where homeowners can practice what they learn.

These smaller farms will serve as an educational, hands-on amenity for families, where residents can learn each stage of growing produce and then put what they learn into practice. An existing program, “Seed the Table,” will allow residents to plant crops, watch them grow and eat what they produced.

The farmers will also work with the lifestyle director to hold chef-led cooking classes at the community’s gathering barn, and will host seasonal celebrations and festivals that bring homeowners together.

Beyond conservation and sustainability, the farm is meant to build community, encourage learning and offer a unique lifestyle.

The Grow, which features homes from the low $400s to the high $800s, isn’t immune to the macro-level sales pressures impacting the local and national housing market. McNamara concedes that his team has to leverage generous incentives like mortgage rate buydowns to finish sales.

However, he also confirmed that The Grow has sold more than 100 homes since opening pre-sales about six months ago, and that prospective buyers have been anticipating the community’s opening for years.

The agrihood concept is the hook that draws buyers in.

“The farm is a big draw,” McNamara said. “I think the agrihood is what’s bringing people in, but what’s getting them to buy is once they see everything else that comes with it.”

In addition to the working farm and conservation space, The Grow will feature a main street with about 170,000 square feet of retail and commercial space, envisioned as a high-end retail and dining corridor that can work as a visitor magnet.

Other features include an indoor event and social space, an outdoor green space with a community pizza oven, a dedicated dog park, walking and nature trails, playgrounds and other resident-only amenities like a clubhouse, a gym and a pool.

“[Buyers] are shocked to find out that this isn’t a little farm community. This is a high-end resort neighborhood.”

The community’s preservation of nature is another big selling point. While many newly built communities lack mature trees and vegetation, The Grow’s preservation space means that residents can live alongside nature from day one.

“It’s really about getting immersed in nature, and that’s really hard to find in new construction,” McNamara said. “Homebuilders typically buy a piece of land and clear it out, start from scratch, and then plant a lot of trees. But you’ve got another 30 years until you can really enjoy the shade from it. In this neighborhood, we’ve left hundreds of 100-year-old oaks all throughout the community. We’ve made them focal points.”

“One of the big draws is the lifestyle and the outdoor feeling that you’re going to have in this community,” he added

Claude AI Predicts and Backs Claver’s $10K XRP Price Prediction as Anthropic Faces Pompliano Backlash

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Brad Garlinghouse wants XRP to become the institutional gold standard for crypto rails, while David Schwartz sees blockchain transforming everyday finance. Now, Claude AI predicts XRP could reach $10,000, backing a bullish scenario popularized by Jake Claver as Anthony Pompliano takes aim at Anthropic’s new financial ambitions.

The prediction arrives as Ripple expands its institutional footprint, with Garlinghouse recently outlining a future where digital assets become central to global financial infrastructure. Schwartz has also envisioned blockchain becoming as accessible as the internet, powering payments, investments, lending, and everyday money management.

Meanwhile, Pompliano accused Anthropic of approaching engineers at his Silvia startup to obtain proprietary information before launching Claude Personal Finance. The controversy has put Claude’s financial ambitions in the spotlight, creating an interesting backdrop for its latest XRP price prediction.

Claude’s bullish case centers on XRP becoming the neutral bridge asset for global financial settlement. Banks, payment providers, and central banks could eventually use XRP to move value across borders instantly instead of maintaining pre-funded accounts across dozens of currencies.

That shift could create enormous demand for XRP as financial institutions build the liquidity needed to support international transactions.

Claude also highlighted XRP’s available supply as a potential catalyst. A significant portion of its supply remains locked in escrow, custody, and long-term wallets. As institutional adoption increases, the tradable float could shrink while demand continues growing.

The resulting supply-demand imbalance could trigger substantial price repricing.

Claude also identified regulatory clarity, tokenized assets, and stablecoins settling on the XRP Ledger as additional catalysts. Combined with growing demand for alternatives to traditional financial infrastructure, these developments could strengthen XRP’s position considerably.

Claver’s $10,000 prediction follows a similar thesis. He has argued that XRP’s role in global liquidity could eventually become vastly more valuable than its current market price suggests.

Garlinghouse’s latest comments add another interesting dimension. At Ripple Swell 2026, he described XRP as a potential institutional gold standard for crypto rails over the next five years. He also discussed moving more traditional financial activity onto blockchain infrastructure.

Caught up with @Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse and David Schwartz at Ripple #Swell to talk the future of @Ripple, institutional utility, and the path ahead for #XRP.Brad breaks down why 2026 has been a massive year for transaction volume growth, and David Schwartz shares his… pic.twitter.com/6zpWZGUcQG

Schwartz’s vision goes further, with blockchain eventually becoming part of how ordinary people manage money, receive payments, invest, lend, and borrow.

While XRP bulls anticipate a transformation in institutional payments, Bitcoin Hyper is targeting another enormous opportunity: bringing high-speed financial applications to the Bitcoin ecosystem.

Bitcoin remains the largest cryptocurrency by market cap, attracting an enormous audience of holders and investors. Bitcoin Hyper aims to tap into that liquidity through a Layer 2 network designed to introduce faster transactions and decentralized finance functionality.

Its architecture uses the Solana Virtual Machine (SVM), bringing Solana-style execution capabilities into a Bitcoin-focused ecosystem.

The ambition? It’s straightforward. Combine Bitcoin’s enormous liquidity and established investor base with transaction speeds designed for modern decentralized applications. Near-instant finality and low transaction costs are central to its pitch.

HYPER is also attracting attention through its presale pricing. The project has reportedly raised more than $33 million, showing considerable early fundraising momentum.

It offers 35% APY staking rewards, another incentive for early participants. Holders can stake their tokens and accumulate additional HYPER rather than simply leaving their allocation idle.

With the planned November 26 launch approaching, the presale is entering an important period. Those looking for exposure to Bitcoin’s expanding Layer 2 ecosystem are anticipating Hyper’s market debut before CEXes list it.

Claude’s $10,000 scenario ultimately comes down to XRP becoming a critical component of international financial infrastructure. Ripple already has a growing institutional network, with more than 85 licenses and partnerships spanning payments, custody, and digital asset infrastructure.

Its recent final approval for a national trust bank charter also strengthens its position within regulated financial markets. Meanwhile, RLUSD continues expanding Ripple’s stablecoin ecosystem, adding another layer to its institutional offering.

Ripple’s more than $16 trillion in transactions enabled across its products during 2025, and it expects to exit 2026 with a $900 million annualized product revenue run rate. These developments give Claver’s long-term thesis an increasingly ambitious backdrop.

If XRP captures a meaningful share of global settlement activity, institutional liquidity requirements could become a major driver of demand. Claude’s analysis highlights that the combination of expanding adoption and a shrinking tradable supply could create the conditions for an extraordinary repricing.

The $10,000 prediction is no longer just about another speculative price target. It is about how much value the global financial system could eventually place on a neutral digital settlement network.

TikTok Search Ads Are Becoming Real Search Advertising via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →
